*shall take another; her food, her clothing, and her marriage rights, he shall not diminish. And if he does not do these three things for her, then she shall go out free without money.* "And when a man sells his daughter to be a maidservant" signifies the affection for truth from natural enjoyment; "she shall not go out as the male servants go" signifies a state unlike truth without affection. "If she is unpleasing in her master's eyes" signifies if the affection for truth from natural enjoyment does not agree with spiritual truth; "so that he will not marry her" signifies that it cannot be conjoined; "then he shall let her be redeemed" signifies alienation from those truths; "he shall have no power to sell her to a foreign people" signifies not to those who are not of the faith of the church; "seeing he has dealt treacherously with her" signifies that doing so is contrary to the laws of Divine order. "And if he has betrothed her to his son" signifies if it agrees with any derived truth so that it can be conjoined to it; "he shall deal with her according to the way of daughters" signifies that it shall be as genuine affection for truth. "If he takes another for himself" signifies conjunction with the affection for truth from another source; "her food, her clothing, and her marriage rights, he shall not diminish" signifies no deprivation of the interior life which is food, nor of the exterior life which is clothing, thus no deprivation of conjunction which is the marriage right. "And if he does not do these three things for her" signifies the deprivation of these; "then she shall go out free without money" signifies alienation from it without truth conjoined to it.

8993. *And when a man sells his daughter to be a maidservant.* That this signifies the affection for truth from natural enjoyment is evident from the meaning of a man's daughter as the affection for truth; for by "daughter" is signified affection (see n. 2362, 3963) and by "man" truth (n. 3134), as also by an Israelite who is meant here.

Therefore, the daughter of a man being sold for a maidservant signifies an affection for truth derived from the enjoyment of natural affection. <margin>**2**</margin> By natural enjoyment is meant the enjoyment flowing from self-love and the love of the world. Those who have an affection for truth derived from those loves are those who learn the doctrines of the church (which are called the truths of faith) either for the sake of gain or for the sake of honors, and not for the sake of life. Such affections for truth, which do not flow from spiritual good but from natural enjoyment, are represented by the daughter of an Israelite man sold to be a handmaid or maidservant; for everything originating from self-love or from the love of the world is not free, but servile. What is meant by free and what is meant by servile may be seen above (n. 892, 905, 1947, 2870-2893, 6205). The nature of affections for truth arising from those loves is described in the internal sense in what follows. It is to be observed that <margin>**3**</margin> genuine affection for truth is the willingness and desire to know the most essential truths of faith for the sake of good use as an end and for the sake of life; but an affection for truth that is not genuine is the willingness and desire for truths for the sake of self—that is, for achieving honors and for winning gain. Those who have an affection for truth from this origin have no concern whether the truths they know are genuine, provided they are such as they can pass off as truths. Therefore, they cling merely to confirming the doctrines of the church in which they were born, whether these are true or not. They are also in darkness regarding the truths themselves, for worldly ends (which are gains) and bodily ends (which are honors) cause utter blindness. But those who have a genuine <margin>**4**</margin> affection for truth—that is, who desire to know truths for the sake of good use and for the sake of life—remain also in the

<meta>This page continues the exposition of Exodus 21:7–11, specifically explaining the symbolic distinction between menservants and maidservants as they relate to the intellectual and voluntary parts of the mind.</meta>

<header>No. 8994.] CHAPTER XXI. VER. 7 — 11.</header>

<margin>4</margin>
The difference between menservants and those who are in affection for truth (who were represented by maidservants) is the same as the difference between *knowing* truth and *willing* truth. To know truth belongs only to the intellectual part, but to will truth belongs to the voluntary part; and thus the difference is like that between knowledge and affection. Those who are in the knowledge of truth and good (and in the representative sense are menservants or men) are not affected by truth and good itself, but are only affected by the knowledge of it; consequently, they are delighted with truths for the sake of knowledge. But those who are in affection for truth and for good (and in the representative sense are maidservants or women) are not affected by knowledge, but by the truths and goods themselves when they hear them and perceive them in others. Such affection is common with good women, while affection for the knowledge of truth is common with men. Therefore, those who are in spiritual perception love women who are affected by truths, but do not love women who are focused on mere knowledges; for it is according to divine order that men should be in knowledges, but women only in affections, and thus that women should not love themselves for their knowledges, but should love men, and from this should come <term>marriage love</term>. For this reason also, the ancients said that women should remain silent in the church. This being the case, knowledges both outward and inward are therefore represented by men, but affections by women; and here, the affections for truth which arise from the enjoyments of natural loves are represented by maidservants. As these are of quite another nature than those who are affected by knowledges, it is altogether different for maidservants than for menservants. This, then, is what is signified by the statute that "the maidservant shall not go out as the menservants go." But it should be known that this is the case with those who belong to the Lord's spiritual kingdom, but it is otherwise with those who belong to His celestial kingdom. In the latter kingdom, husbands are in

8995. *If she is unpleasing in the eyes of her master.* That this signifies if the affection for truth from natural enjoyment does not agree with spiritual truth, is evident from the signification of a maidservant—of whom it is said that she is unpleasing—as affection from natural enjoyment (see n. 8993, 8994); from the signification of "unpleasing," when said of that affection in respect to spiritual truth, as not agreeing with it—of which below; from the signification of "in the eyes," as in the perception (see n. 2829, 3529, 4083, 4339); and from the signification of "master," as
<margin>2</margin> spiritual truth (n. 8981). It must be explained how this is. That a maidservant is the affection for truth from the enjoyments of self-love or of the love of the world was said above (n. 8993, 8994); and that this affection can be conjoined to spiritual truth is evident from this: that affection for spiritual truth is an internal affection, or in the internal man, but affection for truth from natural enjoyment is in the external man. Internal affection, which belongs to the spiritual man, is conjoined continually with external affection, which belongs to the natural man, but still in such a way that the internal affection for truth is the ruling affection and the external affection is subservient; for it is according to Divine order that the spiritual man should rule over the natural (n. 8961, 8967). Moreover, when the spiritual man rules, then a person looks upward, which is represented by having the head in heaven; but when the natural man rules, then a person looks downward, which is re-
<margin>3</margin> presented by having the head in hell. To throw more light on this subject, something further will be said. Most people, by the truths which they learn and the good deeds which they do, think indeed of personal gain in their country or of honor. <meta>This page continues the exposition of Exodus 21:7–11, explaining the relationship between the spiritual and natural man, specifically how natural gains and honors serve as means to spiritual ends.</meta>

For when gain or honor is regarded as a means to an end, and not as the end itself, then the gain or honor is not what is valued, but rather the end, which is use. As, for example, he who acquires and procures riches for himself for the sake of a use which he loves above all things is not then delighted with riches for the sake of riches, but for the sake of uses. Uses indeed constitute the spiritual life of men, and riches are only serviceable as means (see n. 6933—6938). From this it may be seen what the quality of the natural man must be in order that it may be conjoined with the spiritual—namely, that it must regard gains and honors, and thus riches and dignities, as means and not as an end; for what a person regards as an end constitutes his very life, because he loves it above all things, since that which is loved is regarded as an end.

<margin>4</margin>
He who does not know that the end regarded—or, what is the same, the love—constitutes the spiritual life of man, and consequently that a person is where his love is (in heaven if the love is heavenly, in hell if the love is infernal), cannot understand how this is the case. He may guess that the enjoyment of natural loves, which are self-love and the love of the world, cannot agree with spiritual truth and good; for he does not know that a person in the course of regeneration is to be completely inverted, and that when he is inverted his head is in heaven, but until he is inverted his head is in hell. He is with his head in hell when he regards the enjoyments of self-love or of the love of the world as an end, but he is with his head in heaven when those enjoyments are used as means to an end. For the end, which is the love, is the only thing in a person which is living; the means to the end are not living of themselves, but receive life from the end. Therefore means derived from an ultimate end are called mediate ends, and these, so far as they regard the ultimate end...

When a man is of this character, he considers himself as nothing when he looks to the Lord, and the world as nothing as well; and if he considers himself as anything, it is so that he may be able to serve the Lord. Moreover, to betroth maidservants or to have them as concubines was permitted in the representative church—especially the Jewish and Israelite church—for the reason that a wife represented affection for spiritual truth, while a maidservant represented affection for natural truth; thus, the former represented the internal of the church within a person, but the latter represented the external. This was represented by Hagar, who was betrothed to Abraham, and also by the two handmaids who were betrothed to Jacob. From this it is now clear what is meant in the internal representative sense by a maidservant not being betrothed if she is unpleasing: namely, if affection from natural pleasure (which is the maidservant) does not agree with spiritual truth. This disagreement arises chiefly from the fact that the affection represented by the maidservant would rule, and that it was of such a disposition and heart that it could not be bent to love the Lord. That a maidservant is also an affirmative medium serving for the <term>conjunction</term> of the <term>external man</term> and the <term>internal man</term> may be seen above (n. 3913, 3917, 3931).

8996. *So that he will not espouse her.* That this signifies that it cannot be conjoined is evident from the signification of being espoused as being conjoined; for those who are espoused are conjoined. In the <term>internal sense</term>, being espoused properly signifies the agreement of hearts or of minds which precedes the conjunction of marriage; and since agreement in the spiritual world conjoins and disagreement disjoins, therefore being espoused here signifies being conjoined.

8997. *Then he shall let her be redeemed.* That this signifies alienation from those truths is evident from the signification of being redeemed by him who sold her or by another—thus of being sold by the master—as alienation from that spiritual truth. That to be sold is alienation may be seen above (n. 4098, 4752, 4758, 5886), as also that 'master' is spiritual truth (n. 8981, 8995).

8998. *To sell her to a foreign people he shall have no power.* That this signifies not to those who are not of the faith of the church is evident from the signification of a "foreign people" as those who are outside the church, thus who are not of the faith of the church (see n. 2049, 2115, 7996); and from the signification of selling as alienating—concerning which see just above (n. 8997). In regard to this, the case is that those who are born within the church and have been imbued from infancy with the principles of the truth of the church ought not to contract marriages with those who are outside the church and have thus been imbued with things that are not of the church. The reason is that there is no <term>conjunction</term> between them in the spiritual world, for everyone in that world is in <term>consociation</term> according to his good and the truth there—

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Exodus 21, specifically explaining the spiritual prohibitions against selling a Hebrew maidservant to foreigners and the theological basis for marriage within the same faith.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header>EXODUS.</header>
<meta>[No. 8998.</meta>

from; and since there is no <term>conjunction</term> between such in the spiritual world, neither ought there to be any <term>conjunction</term> on earth. For marriages, regarded in themselves, are conjunctions of hearts and of minds, the spiritual life of which is from the truths and goods of faith and of charity. On this account, moreover, marriages on earth between those who are of different religions are accounted in heaven as heinous, and still more so the marriages of those who are of the church with those who are outside of the church. This also was the reason why the Jewish and Israelite nation was forbidden to make marriages with the Gentiles (Deut. 7:3, 4), and why it was altogether heinous to commit sexual immorality with them (Num. 25:1–9). <note>The original text cites Deuteronomy 8:3, 4, though the prohibition against intermarriage is found in Deuteronomy 7.</note>

**2** This appears still clearer from the origin of <term>marriage love</term>, which is from the marriage of good and of truth (n. 2727–2759). When <term>marriage love</term> descends from this source, it is heaven itself in man. This heavenly state is destroyed when two spouses are of unlike heart arising from unlike faith. For this reason, a maidservant of the daughters of Israel—that is, of those who are of the church—was not to be sold to a foreign people—that is, to those who are outside of the church; for these would then espouse her—that is, would be conjoined to her—and would thus profane the things which are of the church; therefore it is said that this is to deal treacherously.

8999. *Seeing he has dealt treacherously with her.* That this signifies that doing so is contrary to the laws of Divine order is evident from the meaning of "dealing treacherously" as being contrary to Divine truth, or what is the same, contrary to the laws of Divine order; that this is signified by dealing treacherously is plain from what was presented just above (n. 8998). The laws of Divine order are truths in heaven, for the Divine order is from the Divine truth which proceeds from the Lord (n. 8700, 8988). To "deal treacherously" is a common form of speaking in the Word, signifying in the <term>internal sense</term> to act contrary to the truth

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Exodus 21, explaining that acting contrary to the truths and goods of heaven is contrary to Divine order. It specifically addresses the spiritual meaning of a master betrothing a maidservant to his son.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>No. 9001.] CHAPTER XXI. VER. 7—11.</header>

and good in heaven, or what is the same, contrary to Divine order; as in Isaiah (21:2; 33:1; 48:8) and elsewhere (Jer. 3:20; 5:11; 12:1, 6; Hosea 5:7; 6:7; Mal. 2:10, 11, 14, 15; Ps. 78:57; 119:158).

9000. *And if he has espoused her to his son.* That this signifies if it agrees with any derived truth so that it can be conjoined to it, is evident from the signification of a son, as truth (see n. 489, 491, 533, 1147, 2623, 2803, 2813, 3373, 3704, 4257), here derived truth, because by the master who is the father is signified the principal truth from which the rest are derived (n. 8981); and from the signification of espousing, as being conjoined—of which was spoken above (n. 8996).

9001. *He shall deal with her according to the manner of daughters.* That this signifies that it shall be as genuine affection for truth, is evident from the signification of "according to the manner," as with the same right; for by the manner [*judicium*] is signified external truth or right, such as exists in the civil state within the representative church (n. 8972); and from the signification of daughter, as affection for truth (n. 2362, 3024, 3963), here genuine affection for truth, since by maidservant is signified affection for truth from natural enjoyment (n. 8993), thus not genuine until she is espoused either to her master or to his son. But when she is espoused—that is, when there is conjunction with spiritual truth, which is signified by master and by son—then the affection for truth becomes as genuine; for then the natural is subordinated to spiritual truth, and when it is subordinated it no longer exists in its own right, but in the right of the spiritual truth under which it is subordinated. Therefore the natural becomes as spiritual, because it acts as one with it. And then also the life of spiritual truth is transferred into the natural and gives it life. But espousal or conjunction with a maidservant differs from conjunction with a daughter in this: that the latter conjunction is effected in the internal man, but the former in the external.

<summary>This section explains that when natural affection for truth is conjoined to spiritual truth, it is elevated to the status of a "daughter," meaning it becomes genuine. This process subordinates the natural mind to the spiritual, allowing spiritual life to flow into and animate the external man.</summary>

9002. *If he has taken another for himself.* That this signifies conjunction with affection for truth from another source is evident from the signification of taking or espousing another as being conjoined (see n. 8996); for marriage, which is here meant by espousal, is in the spiritual sense the conjunction of the life of one with the life of another. According to Divine order, there is a conjunction of life from the truths of faith with life from the good of charity. From this comes all spiritual conjunction, from which natural conjunction exists as its origin. By "taking another" is signified conjunction with affection for truth from another source, for the maidservant previously spoken of is affection for truth from natural enjoyment (n. 8993); therefore "another" is affection for truth from another source.

<margin>2</margin>What affection from another source is may be known from this: that every affection belonging to love is of the widest extension—so wide, indeed, as to exceed all human understanding. The human understanding does not even go so far as to know the categories of the varieties of affection, much less the species of those categories, and even less the particulars and individual elements of those particulars. For whatever is in man, especially that which belongs to affection or love, is of infinite variety. This may be clearly evident from the fact that affection for good and truth, which belongs to love for the Lord and love toward the neighbor, constitutes the whole of heaven; and yet all who are in the heavens, where there are myriads, differ from one another as to good, and would differ even if they were multiplied to innumerable myriads of myriads. For there cannot be a single thing in the universe that is completely like another while also remaining distinct; it must be varied—that is, different from another—in order to be anything by itself (see n. 684, 690, 3241, 3744, 3745, 3986, 4005, 4149, 5598, 7236, 7833, 7836, 8003). From these points it may be known in some measure what is meant by an affection from another source, namely, an affection which

It emphasizes that human affections and the varieties of good in heaven are infinitely diverse, ensuring that every individual remains distinct.</summary>

Such affections, which are represented by maidservants espoused to one man, are of one genus, but there is a difference between them in species, which is called a specific difference. These things might be illustrated by various examples, but the general idea derived from what has been already said will be sufficient. That the conjunctions and subordinations of such affections <margin>3</margin> under one spiritual truth might be represented, it was permitted to the Israelite and Jewish nation to have several concubines—as to Abraham (Gen. xxv. 6), also to David, Solomon, and others. For whatever was permitted to that nation was for the sake of representation, namely, that by external things they might represent the internal things of the church (n. 3246). But when the internal things of the church were revealed by the Lord, then the representations of internal things by external ceased, because then the internal things were those which the person of the church was to take on, and by which he was to worship the Lord, which are the things of faith and love. Therefore it was then no longer permitted to have more wives than one, nor to take concubines for wives (n. 865, 2727-2759, 3246, 4837).

9003. *Her food, her raiment, and her duty of marriage, shall he not diminish.* That this signifies no deprivation of the interior life which is food, nor of the exterior life which is clothing, thus no deprivation of conjunction which is the duty of marriage, is evident from the signification of food, as the support of the interior life—for food, or solid food and drink, in the spiritual sense is the knowledges of good and of truth, solid food being the knowledges of good (n. 5147), and drink the knowledges of truth (n. 3168, 3772), therefore food stands for what nourishes the spiritual life of man (n. 5293, 5576, 5579, 5915, 8562); from the signification of raiment or clothing, as the support of the exterior life, for raiment or clothing in the spiritual sense is the lower

It is the same with a person's affection as it is with the person himself: unless it is supported by food, it dies. Indeed, a person in his inner self is nothing but affection: a good person is affection for good and for the truth derived from it, while an evil person is affection for evil and for the falsity derived from it. This is especially clear when a person becomes a spirit, for the sphere of life that then flows from him is a sphere of either affection for good or affection for evil. His nourishment or support then comes not from natural food and drink, but from spiritual food, which is falsity from evil for an evil spirit and truth from good for a good spirit. The nourishment for human minds while they live in the body in the world is no different. For this reason, all things related to food—such as bread, meat, wine, water, and many other things—signify things of spiritual nourishment in the spiritual sense of the Word. <margin>3</margin> From this it is also clear what is meant by the words of the Lord in Matthew: *“Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds out of the mouth of God”* (4:4); also by His words in Luke: *“You shall eat and drink at My table in My kingdom”* (22:30); and again in Matthew: *“I say to you, I will not drink from now on of this fruit of the vine, until that day when I drink it new with you in My Father’s kingdom”* (26:29). From this it may be clearly known what is meant by the flesh and by the blood of the Lord in the sixth chapter of John (*v. 49-58*), and also by these words in the same chapter: *My flesh is food indeed, and My blood is drink indeed* (verse 55). That flesh in the Word is the good of love may be seen above (n. 3813, 7850), and also that blood is the good of faith (n. 4735, 6978, 7317, 7326, 7846, 7850, 7877); in the same way bread and wine (n. 2165, 2177, 3464, 3478, 3735, 3813, 4211, 4217, 4735, 4976, 5915, 6118, 6377).

9004. *If he shall not have done these three unto her.* That this signifies the deprivation of those things is evident without further explanation.

9005. *Then shall she go out free without silver.* That this signifies alienation from them without truth conjoined to it, is evident from the signification of "going out"—here from service and from coupling—as the abandonment of her master-husband [*dominus vir*], thus alienation; and from the signification of "free without silver," as being without truth conjoined to it—that silver stands for truth may be seen above (n. 1551, 2954, 5658, 6112, 6914, 6917, 8932). How this is may be seen from what was shown just above (n. 9003), namely, that natural affection conjoined to spiritual truth, which is signified by a maidservant betrothed to a son, cannot survive at all without support from spiritual truth. Therefore, if it is not supported, the conjunction is dissolved, and consequently there is alienation. The reason why this is the result without truth from that source conjoined to it, is that then it is associated with another, which cannot happen with the life derived from truth from another source. Such is the signification of the above words, because such is the case with associations in the spiritual world.

And he who strikes his father and his mother, dying he shall die.* "He who strikes a man, so that he die" signifies the injury of the truth of faith and thereby the loss of spiritual life; "dying he shall die" signifies damnation. "And if one has not lain in wait" signifies when it was not from foresight of the will; "but God has caused it to happen to his hand" signifies appearing as if by chance; "then I will appoint you a place where he shall flee" signifies the state of being blameless and thus exempt from punishment. "And he who strikes his father and his mother" signifies the blaspheming of the Lord and of His kingdom; "dying he shall die" signifies damnation.

9007. *He who strikes a man, so that he die.* That this signifies the injury of the truth of faith and thereby the loss of spiritual life, is evident from the signification of striking, as injuring by falsity (see n. 7136, 7146); from the signification of a man [*vir*], as the truth of faith — of which follows below; and from the signification of dying, as the loss of spiritual life (n. 5407, 6119, 7494); for in the internal sense no other life is meant, while in the external sense the natural life is meant. The reason why spiritual life perishes by the injury of the truth of faith is that good united to truth constitutes that life; and therefore when truth is snatched away, good falls away, and thus spiritual life. <meta>This page continues the spiritual explanation of Exodus 21:12, specifically detailing why "man" signifies the intellectual faculty and the truth of faith in the spiritual sense, and how "death" signifies spiritual damnation.</meta>

<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>No. 9008.] CHAPTER XXI. VER. 12-15.</header>

In heaven, no attention is paid to a person nor to anything regarding a person, but rather to things abstracted from the person (n. 4380, 8343, 8985). Therefore, there they do not perceive a man when "man" is named in the Word, for a man is a person; but instead of the person, they perceive that faculty of his by virtue of which he is a man—namely, the intellectual faculty. And when they perceive this faculty, they perceive the truth of faith; for the truth of faith belongs to it and not only enlightens it, but also forms it. And just as the intellectual part of man is perceived in heaven by the word man [*vir*], so his voluntary faculty is perceived by the word man [*homo*, a human being], because a human being is a human being [*homo*] from the will, but is a man [*vir*] from the understanding. And since the will is the man himself, the good of love is therefore the man; for this belongs to the will and perfects and forms it. That "man" [*vir*] is the intellectual faculty and therefore the truth of faith may be seen above (n. 158, 265, 749, 1007, 2517, 3134, 3309, 3459, 4823, 7716); also that "man" [*homo*] is the good of love (n. 768, 4287, 7523, 8547, 8988).

9008. *Dying he shall die.* That this signifies damnation is evident from the signification of death by "dying" as damnation (see n. 5407, 6119, 7494). Death is damnation because in those who are damned, the truths of faith and the goods of love have been extinguished; for these are what constitute the very life of man, since they are from the Lord, who is the only source of life. When these are extinguished, falsities and evils take their place; which, since they are opposite to the truths and goods that belong to life, are therefore of death—specifically spiritual death, which is damnation, hell, and eternal unhappiness. That those who are in evils and falsities, or who are in hell, still live is because they were born as human beings and therefore with the faculty of receiving life from the Lord. Indeed, they receive just enough life from the Lord to be able to think, reason, and speak, and thereby to cause evil in themselves to appear as good and falsity as truth, and thus to live a semblance of life.

<vocab>vir, homo, intellectual faculty, voluntary faculty, truth of faith, good of love, damnation, spiritual death, semblance of life</vocab>

<summary>In heaven, the word "man" refers abstractly to the intellectual and voluntary faculties rather than a person. Spiritual death or damnation is defined as the extinction of truth and good, though the damned maintain a semblance of life through their innate faculty to receive life from the Lord.</summary>

9009. *And if one has not lain in wait.* That this signifies when it was not from foresight originating in the will, is evident from the signification of lying in wait, as acting with deliberation, thus with foresight; for the evil which one who lies in wait is about to do, he foresees in his mind. And because he does such evil with foresight, he also does it from the will, for it proceeds from there. There are evils which proceed from the will of man but are not from foresight, and there are evils which proceed from the will and are from foresight. Those which proceed from the will and from foresight are much worse than those which are not from foresight; for man sees that they are evils, and can therefore desist from them, but is not willing, and by this he confirms them in himself. Evils that are confirmed infect the nature so that afterward they can scarcely be extirpated; for in such a case he summons spirits from hell who
<margin>2</margin> afterward do not easily retire. Evils which proceed from one part of the mind and not at the same time from the other—such as those which come from the <term>intellectual part</term> and not at the same time from the <term>voluntary part</term>—are not rooted in and appropriated to man. That alone is rooted in and appropriated to him which passes from the <term>intellectual part</term> into the <term>voluntary part</term>, or what is the same, which passes from the thought (which belongs to the understanding) into the affection (which belongs to the will), and from there into action. Those things which enter into the will are what are said to
<margin>3</margin> enter into the heart. But evils which proceed only from the will, and thus not with premeditation, are such as man inclines to hereditarily, or from some previous actual doing of evil from it. These are not <term>imputed</term> to man unless he has confirmed them in his <term>intellectual part</term> (see n. 966, 2308, 8806); but when they are confirmed in this part, they are then inscribed on the man and become his own and are <term>imputed</term> to him. However, those evils cannot be confirmed with man in his <term>intellectual part</term> except in his adult age, namely, when he begins to think for himself and to

<summary>This passage explains that "lying in wait" signifies premeditated evil, which is more spiritually damaging because it is confirmed by both the intellect and the will. It distinguishes between hereditary inclinations and confirmed evils, noting that only what is intellectually sanctioned in adulthood is fully appropriated and imputed to an individual.</summary>

9010. *But God has caused it to happen to his hand.* That this signifies an appearance of chance is evident from the idea concerning chance among the ancients, which was that it happened from God; therefore they expressed the idea of chance by the phrase, "God caused it to happen to the hand." For those who belonged to the Ancient Churches knew that the providence of the Lord was in each and every thing, and that things which happened—that is, things which appeared to be by chance—were of His providence. Therefore the simple, who could not distinguish between things that were of permission and those that were of good pleasure, attributed both good and evil to the Lord: good because they knew that all good is from Him, but evil because of the appearance. For when a person does evil and thereby turns himself away from the Lord, it appears as if the Lord turns Himself away, for then the Lord appears to him behind and not in front. Therefore it is that if anyone struck another by chance, and thus without will or foresight, it was expressed this way: that God caused it to happen to the hand. That the providence of the Lord is in each and every thing has been shown before (see n. 1919, 4329, 5122, 5155, 5195, 5894, 6058, 6481–6487, 6489, 6491, 7004, 7007, 8478, 8717), as well as that things that happen or are of chance are of providence (n. 5508, 6493, 6494), and that evil is attributed to the Lord even though it is actually from man (n. 2447, 5798, 6071, 6832, 6991, 6997, 7533, 7877, 7926, 8197, 8227, 8228, 8282, 8284, 8483, 8632).

To signify this, such an error or accident is described by an instrument of any sort and by a stone which he threw at his neighbor so that he died, and likewise by an axe or its iron head falling from its handle while they were both cutting wood in the forest. The reason it is described by such things is that an instrument signifies a matter of knowledge, a stone signifies the truth of faith (and in the opposite sense, falsehood), and the iron of an axe signifies the same; and cutting wood signifies disputation concerning good based on one’s religion.

<margin>4</margin> Anyone can see that homicide committed through error would not have been described without a hidden reason by the iron of an axe falling from its handle in a forest, since such a mishap can rarely happen, and hardly once in the course of many years. But such a mishap is described this way on account of the internal sense, which describes the injury to a soul by another person through the falsehoods of faith which, based on his religion, he had believed to be truths; for he who does an injury by means of falsehoods which he believes to be truths does it not on purpose, but from a better conscience, because he does it from faith and the resulting zeal for his religion. To signify these things in the internal sense, they are described by those who slay their neighbors by accident, as said above: by a stone, by cutting wood in a forest, and the iron of an axe then falling from the handle upon a neighbor. For a stone is the truth of faith in the natural man, and in the opposite sense, falsehood (see n. 643, 1298, 3720, 6426, 8609, 8941), as does iron (n. 425, 426). The iron of an axe falling from its wooden handle is truth separated from good, for the wooden handle stands for good (n. 643, 2812, 3720, 8354); cutting wood stands for placing merit in works (n. 1110, 4943, 8740). *his border, the forest of his Carmel (37:24). In Jeremiah: I will visit you according to the fruit of your doings . . . and I will kindle a fire in her forest (21:14). In Ezekiel: Prophesy against the forest of the field in the South; and say to the forest of the South . . . Behold, I will kindle a fire in you, and it shall devour every tree (20:46, 47). And in Micah: Feed Your people with Your rod, the flock of Your heritage which dwell solitarily, in the forest in the midst of Carmel (7:14). Who does not see that in these passages a forest does not mean a literal forest, and that Lebanon and Carmel, which are forests, do not mean Lebanon and Carmel themselves, but something of the church? Yet what aspect of the church is meant has been hidden until now, because the <term>internal sense</term> lies hidden. It is remarkable that in such a learned world as Europe—which, above all others, possesses the Word (where every detail has an internal sense)—the very knowledge of that sense is missing. Yet this knowledge existed among the ancients in Chaldea, Assyria, Egypt, Arabia, and from there in Greece, in whose books, emblems, and hieroglyphics such things are still to be found. The reason why such knowledge has been lost is that there is no faith that the spiritual is anything at all.*

9012. *And when a man shall have dealt purposely against his companion.* This signifies premeditation from a depraved will. This is evident from the meaning of "dealing purposely" as being with premeditation; for whoever intends evil within himself does it with premeditation. Since it is evil and he does it, it comes from the will, for the origin of doing evil is in the will. However, the falsity by which evil is affirmed, defended, and promoted belongs to the thought, and thus comes from a depraved or inverted understanding. That a person is guilty when they do evil from both—namely, from the understanding and from the will—can be seen above (n. 9009).

9013. *To slay him with guile.* This signifies malice

Evils are done either from enmity, or from hatred, or from revenge, and either with guile or without guile. <margin>2</margin>

Those who in the world have ensnared their neighbor with guile regarding worldly and earthly things, in the other life ensnare their neighbor with guile regarding spiritual and heavenly things; and because they do this in secret, they are committed to the hells behind the back at a depth according to the malice and hurtfulness of the guile, and are thus separated from those who are in front; the latter are called spirits, but the former are called genii (n. 5035, 5977, 8593, 8622, 8625). Genii are not admitted to man as spirits are, because they flow into the affections of the will, acting against the good of love and charity so clandestinely that the influence cannot be perceived at all, and in that way they destroy the truth of faith. In their own hells they make themselves invisible before their companions; for those who have dealt secretly in the world can make themselves invisible in the other life, and when they appear, they appear among themselves as men. [This follows the] paragraph above (n. 9013), namely, that guile in spiritual things—that is, hypocrisy—cannot be forgiven. The reason is that guile is like poison, for it penetrates even to the interiors and kills everything of faith and charity. It destroys *remains*, which are the truths and goods of faith and charity stored up by the Lord in the interiors of man; when these are destroyed, nothing of spiritual life any longer survives. Concerning remains, see what has been said above (n. 468, 530, 560-563, 660, 661, 798, 1050, 1738, 1906, 2284, 5135, 5342, 5344, 5897, 5898, 6156, 7560, 7564). Therefore, when such persons beg the Lord for forgiveness and promise repentance—which is signified by "fleeing to an altar"—they beg and promise nothing at all from the heart, but only from the mouth. Therefore they are not heard, for the Lord looks on the heart and does not regard utterances removed and separated from the heart. Therefore for such people there is no forgiveness, because no repentance <margin>3</margin> finds a place in them. It is believed by many people within the church that the remission of sins is the wiping out and washing away of them, as filth is washed away by water, and that after remission they go on their way clean and pure. Such an opinion prevails especially with those who ascribe everything of salvation to faith alone. But it is to be known that the case is altogether different with the remission of sins. The Lord remits everyone's sins, since He is mercy itself. Nevertheless, they are not thereby remitted unless a person performs serious repentance, turns away from evils, and afterward lives a life of faith and charity, and continues this even to the end of his life. When this is done, then the person receives from the Lord spiritual life, which is called new life. When from this new life he views the evils of his former life and turns away from them and regards them with horror, then for the first time are the evils remitted; for then the person is held in truths and goods by the Lord and is withheld from evils. From this it is plain what the remission of sins is, and that it cannot be granted within an hour, nor a

<summary>This text explains that hypocrisy destroys the internal "remains" of good and truth, making forgiveness impossible because the heart is uninvolved. 9020. *Dying he shall die.* That this signifies damnation may be seen above (n. 9008). Those who wish to distribute by their own will the things that belong to the Lord—especially those concerning the heavenly life of man—for the purpose of gaining dominion over all people and thus winning the world, are thieves in the spiritual sense when they draw confirmation from the Lord's words; for they steal truths from the Word and apply them to evil purposes. These purposes are evil because they have dominion and gain as their end, and not the salvation of souls. If these same persons, without any regard to dominion and gain, have previously acknowledged the truths from the Word which they now apply to evils, they profane them; for in this way they mix falsities from evils with truths. Such people cannot in any way escape damnation, for by doing so they deprive themselves of all spiritual life. That they deprive themselves of spiritual life is plain from this: when they are left to themselves and think from their own perspectives and talk among themselves, they have no faith in truths and do not believe in the Lord, nor in a heaven or a hell. 9021. *And he who curses his father and his mother.* That this signifies the total denial of the Lord and of His kingdom by those who belong to the church, and thus the profanation of the good and truth of the church, is evident from the signification of *cursing*, as aversion and separation (see n. 245, 379, 1423, 3530, 3584, 5071), and therefore also total denial; for whoever turns away and separates themselves from the Lord denies Him in their heart; and from the signification of *father and mother*, as the Lord and His kingdom, and in the relative sense, the good and truth that come from the Lord (n. 8897, 9015). For this reason, denial of the Lord is not profanation for those who are outside the church, such as the Gentiles, Mohammedans, and Jews.

9024. *And when men shall dispute.* That this signifies contention among themselves concerning truths, is evident from the signification of disputing, as contending—of which below; and from the signification of men, as those who are intelligent and who are in truths, and in an abstract sense intellectual things and truths (see n. 3134, 9007). Therefore the disputing of men signifies contention about truths among those who are of the church, and in an abstract sense about truths among themselves. For to dispute in the spiritual sense is to contend about such things as belong to the church, consequently such as belong to faith. Nothing else is meant in the Word by disputing, for the Word is spiritual and treats of spiritual things, that is, of those things which belong to the Lord, of His kingdom in heaven, and of His kingdom on the earth, that is, His church. That disputing signifies in the Word contention about truths and in general in favor of truths against falsities, likewise also defense and liberation from falsities,
<margin>2</margin> is plain from the following passages—in Jeremiah: *A tumult has come even to the end of the earth; for Jehovah has a controversy against the nations, he will plead with all flesh; as for the wicked, he will give them to the sword. . . . Behold, evil shall go forth from nation to nation, and a great tempest shall be raised up from the outermost parts of the earth* (xxv. 31, 32). <margin>3</margin> Again in the same: *Jehovah . . . shall thoroughly plead their cause, that He may give rest to the earth* (50:34) — where pleading or disputing their cause stands for defending truths against falsities and liberating; the earth stands for the church, which has rest when it is in good and thereby in truths. <margin>2</margin> with full force by means of falsities from evil. What is meant by invalidating any truth of the church by external or common truth shall be explained. By external truths are meant truths which are from the literal sense of the Word. Common truths from that sense are such as are received among people generally and therefore are in common speech. There are very many such truths, and they prevail with much force. But the literal sense of the Word is for the simple, for those who are being initiated into the interior truths of faith, and for those who do not understand interior things; for this sense is written according to appearances in the sight of the sensual man, and thus according to his understanding. Therefore it is that in that sense things frequently appear dissimilar and, as it were, contradictory to each other—as for example, that the Lord leads into temptation and again that He does not lead into temptation; that the Lord repents and again that He does not repent; that the Lord acts from anger and wrath and again that He acts from pure clemency and mercy; that souls come to judgment immediately after death and again that it is at the time of the final judgment, and so on. Such truths, since they are from the literal sense of the Word, are called external truths [<term>scientifica</term>], and differ from the truths of faith which are of the doctrine of the

It should be known that the true doctrine of the church is what is called here the internal sense; for in the internal sense there are truths such as the angels have in heaven.

<margin>3</margin>
Among priests and among the people of the church, there are those who teach and learn the truths of the church from the literal sense of the Word, and there are those who teach and learn from doctrine drawn from the Word, which is called the church's doctrine of faith. The latter differ very much from the former in their perception, but they cannot be distinguished by the common people because they both speak from the Word in nearly the same way. But those who teach and learn only the literal sense of the Word without the church's doctrine as a guide understand only those things that relate to the natural or the external man; whereas those who teach and learn from true doctrine drawn from the Word also understand things that belong to the spiritual or internal man. The reason is that the Word in its external or literal sense is natural, but in its internal sense it is spiritual. In the Word, the former sense is called a cloud, but the latter sense is called the glory in the cloud (n. 5922, 6343, 6752, 8106, 8781).

9026. *And he die not.* That this signifies "and it is not extinguished" is evident from the meaning of dying, which is to cease being as one was before (see n. 494, 6587, 6593); consequently, to be extinguished—in this case, *not* to be extinguished. Because the subject here in the internal sense is the agreement of the truths of faith with the truths of the literal sense of the Word, and because the truths of the literal sense of the Word cannot be extinguished since they are truths in the ultimate of order, the text speaks not of the striking of a man from which he dies, but only of the striking of a man from which he does not die. For the truths of the literal sense of the Word may indeed be invalidated, but they cannot be extinguished. Moreover, after they have been invalidated, they may be set aside; but they may be restored again by the unfolding of their meaning. These things are signified by what was ordained concerning a man struck by his companion who rises again and walks upon his staff.

<margin>2</margin> Anyone who thoroughly examines the internal contents of the Word may see that for a hidden reason—which is not grasped by the understanding unless it is enlightened by the light of heaven—it was ordained by the Lord that the striker should be guiltless if the person struck rose again from his bed and walked outside upon his staff. It was especially ordained by the Lord that he who strikes his servant, and the servant does not die within a day or two, should not be punished because he is "his silver," even though this involves taking a man’s life, for the servant is a man, even if a servant. But the hidden reason why it was so ordained by the Lord is not clear except through the internal sense, in which the subject is the truths of the church derived from the Word. Therefore, such things were ordained and indeed commanded as have no validity as laws since the internal things of the church have been opened and revealed by the Lord; for since that time, man is to live an internal life, which is a life of faith and of charity, and such an external life as internal things create.

9027. *But lie down on his bed.* This signifies what is separate in the natural, as is evident from the meaning of lying down as being separated; and from the meaning of a bed as the natural (see n. 6188, 6226, 6463). How this is the case will be made clear from what follows.

For a rod signifies the power which belongs to truth, and thus strength (n. 4876, 4936, 6947, 7011, 7026), and a staff signifies the same, but specifically applied to those who are not in good health. In this sense it is also used in David: *They came upon me in the day of my calamity: but Jehovah was my staff. He brought me forth into a large place* (Ps. xviii. 18, 19)—where the "day of calamity" stands for a weak state in respect to the faith of truth; "Jehovah being a staff" stands for power at that time; and "bringing forth into a large or broad place" means into the truths which belong to faith. Interiorly viewed, they all agree. This may be illustrated by what is said in the Word concerning the sun—that it rises and sets—when yet it neither rises nor sets. Instead, such an appearance is presented to the inhabitants of the earth because the earth rotates every day on its axis. This natural truth lies hidden within the former, which is based on the appearance to the external sight. If it were expressed in the Word contrary to that appearance, common people would not understand it; and what common people do not understand, they do not believe. The case is similar with respect to the Sun of heaven, which is the Lord. It is also said of Him that He rises, but He rises in hearts when a person is being regenerated, and also when he is in the good of love and faith; and He sets when a person is in evil and in the falsity resulting from it. And yet the Lord is continually rising, which is why He is called the Dayspring or Sun-rising; He is never setting, nor does He turn Himself away from man, but man turns himself away from Him. From this arises the appearance that the Lord turns away His face and also brings evil; this is indeed why it is said so in the Word. This is likewise truth, but apparent truth, and thus not opposed to the former. From these things it may now be clear what is meant in the internal sense by "he shall surely heal"—namely, the restoration of spiritual truth, which is accomplished by a right interpretation of external truth, or of the literal sense of the Word.

<margin>2</margin>
It is similar with every truth of the literal sense, for such truth in natural light—which belongs to the sensual man—is seen just as it is expressed in the Word, since the literal sense is natural and intended for the sensual man. But when the same is presented in the light of heaven, it is then seen according to the internal sense, for this sense is spiritual and intended for the heavenly man. This is because those things belonging to natural light vanish in the light of heaven; for natural light is like a shadow or cloud, and heavenly light is like glory and brightness when the cloud is removed.

9033. The weakening of spiritual truth—that is, the truth of the church's doctrine of faith—was the subject of the two verses above; but in the two verses that now follow, the subject is the weakening of external truth, which is the truth of the literal sense of the Word. This truth indeed appears like the former, but it is still not the same; so here also, let an example serve for clarification. It is a spiritual truth, or a genuine truth of the church's doctrine of faith, that the Lord punishes no one, because He is mercy itself. Therefore, whatever He does, He does from mercy, and by no means from anger or revenge. And yet the Lord says in Matthew: *Do not be afraid of those who are able to kill the body . . . but rather fear Him who is able to destroy both body and soul in Gehenna (10:28)*. Here it is said of God that He is to be feared because He is able to destroy body and soul in Gehenna, even though He actually destroys no one. Nevertheless, this is a truth; and therefore it is not to be destroyed—that is, denied. For if it is denied, faith in the Word perishes; and if faith in the Word perishes, a person cannot live spiritually, for a person has spiritual life through faith from the Word. The situation in this matter is this: it is a law of Divine **2** order that good should have its own reward within itself, which is heaven. Therefore, it is also a law that evil has its own punishment within itself, which is hell. The first law is from the Lord, because the Lord wills good for everyone; but the latter law is not

Yet this is attributed to the Lord in the literal sense of the Word because it appears that way. Therefore, because it is an apparent truth, it must not be denied—that is, extinguished—for in that case, faith in the Word would be extinguished, which is the faith of the simple (see n. 2447, 6071, 6991, 6997, 7533, 7632, 7643, 7679, 7710, 7877, 7926, 8197, 8227, 8228, 8282, 8483, 8631, 8632, 9010).

9034. *And when a man strikes his manservant or his maidservant with a rod.* That this signifies whoever within the church ill-treats external truth or the affection for it by their own power, is evident from the meaning of striking as ill-treating, for striking is predicated of any injury whatsoever; from the meaning of a man (here a man of the sons of Israel) as one who is of the church and therefore in spiritual truth, which is the truth of the church's doctrine of faith from the Word (see n. 6426, 6637, 6862, 6868, 7035, 7062, 7198, 7201, 7215, 7223, 7957, 8234, 8805); from the meaning of a manservant as external truth, which is the truth of the Word, but of its literal sense—of which see below; from the meaning of a maidservant as natural affection, thus affection for outward knowledges, because these are on the natural plane (n. 1895, 2567, 3835, 3849, 8993, 8994); and from the meaning of a rod as natural power (n. 4876, 4936, 6947, 7011, 7026), in this case, one’s own power, because the manservant of whom it is said was bought. From this it is plain that by the words, "when a man strikes his manservant or his maidservant," is signified whoever within the church ill-treats the external truth of the <margin>**2**</margin> Word or the affection for it. The reason why a manservant is the truth of the literal sense of the Word is that a servant in general signifies lower or exterior things, since these serve higher or interior things (n. 2541,

<summary>The author explains that apparent truths in the Bible must be maintained to preserve the faith of simple believers, and begins interpreting Exodus 21:20, where striking a servant symbolizes ill-treating external truths or natural affections using one's own power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Exodus 21:20, specifically regarding the relationship between the natural and spiritual levels of the Word and how the literal sense serves as a foundation for the internal sense.</meta>

<page-num>271</page-num>
<header>No. 9035.] CHAPTER XXI. VER. 18—21.</header>

5161, 5164, 5936, 7143). Therefore, a servant signifies the natural level of the mind, since this serves the spiritual (n. 3019, 3020, 5305, 7998). Consequently, it signifies external truth, which belongs to the literal sense of the Word, because this serves spiritual truth, which belongs to the internal sense.

<margin>3</margin>The truth of the internal sense of the Word is the same as the genuine truth of the church's doctrine of faith. How the truth of the literal sense of the Word serves spiritual truth will be briefly explained. A person of the church first learns truth from the literal sense of the Word, which is general truth adapted to the understanding of the external person, who is in natural light. This truth is received through an external path—that is, by hearing—and is stored in the memory of the external person, where various types of knowledge derived from the world are also kept (n. 2469-2494). Afterward, the things stored in this memory are submitted to the sight or view of the internal person, who sees by the light of heaven. From that memory, the internal person selects the truths that agree with the good that flows in from the Lord by way of the soul, and which the person had received. There, the Lord joins truths to good. The truths which are thus joined in the internal person are called spiritual truths, and the good with which the truths are joined is called spiritual good. This good, formed by truths, is what constitutes a person’s spiritual life. The truths themselves are there called truths of faith, and the good is called the good of charity. The good in which truths have thus been implanted is the church within the person.

<margin>4</margin>From this, it is clear how the truths of the literal sense of the Word serve to form spiritual truths—and in general, to form the faith and charity that constitute spiritual life. For spiritual life consists in being moved by truths for the sake of good, being moved by good because of truths, and finally being moved by truths from a state of good.

For the truths of the literal sense of the Word, stored in the natural memory of a person, form a kind of field for the view of the internal person, into which light from heaven flows. From this field, the internal person selects such things as agree with the good within them, as was said above—similar to how the eye selects from a garden things that serve the needs of its life.

9036. *With avenging, vengeance shall be taken.* That this signifies the punishment of death is clear from the meaning of "avenging" or "taking vengeance" as the punishment of death—here, the punishment of spiritual death, which is damnation. For the truth of the literal sense of the Word is extinguished, and with it, faith in the Word. How this occurs may be seen above (n. 9033) and also below (n. 9039).

9037. *Notwithstanding, if he shall continue a day or two.* That this signifies a state of life continuing even to fullness is clear from the meaning of "a day" as a state of life (see n. 893, 2788, 3785, 4850); and from the meaning of "two days" as a succeeding state (n. 1335), thus a full state, namely, of intuition. For when the Word mentions one day, or one week, or one month, or one year, an entire period of time or a state is signified (n. 2906); and when "or two days" is added, it signifies a progression even to fullness.

9038. *Vengeance shall not be taken.* That this signifies no punishment of death is clear from the meaning of "vengeance being taken" as the punishment of death (see above, n. 9036).

9039. *For he is his silver.* That this signifies what is acquired from the proprium, is evident from the meaning of silver as truth (see n. 1551, 2954, 5658, 6112, 6914, 6917); in this case, because it refers to a purchased slave, truth acquired by the proprium is meant. That which is called truth acquired by the proprium is what is believed to be truth through reasoning from principles conceived from the proprium, and yet is not truth. Such is the truth among those who explain the Word without being enlightened by the light of heaven; that is, those who do not read it from an affection for truth for the sake of the good of life, for they are not enlightened. If this truth is extinguished after full inspection, there is no punishment of death (that is, damnation), because it is not Divine spiritual truth; but if it is extinguished before full inspection, there is damnation, for there is a rejection of the truth of faith itself. For what has become a part of anyone’s faith, even if it is not true, ought not to be rejected except by a full examination; if it is rejected sooner, the very beginning of his spiritual life is uprooted. Therefore the Lord also never breaks such truth in a person, but as far as possible bends it. Let an example serve <margin>2</margin> for illustration. A person who believes that the glory and therefore the joy of heaven consist in dominion over many, and who explains the Lord’s words from this accepted principle—concerning the servants who gained ten pounds and five pounds respectively, that they should have power over ten cities and over five cities (Luke 19:11), and also the Lord’s words to the disciples, that they should sit upon thrones and judge the twelve tribes of Israel (Luke 22:30)—if he extinguishes his faith, which is the faith of truth from the literal sense of the Word, before full examination, he causes the loss of his spiritual life. But if, after a full view of the subject, he interprets these words of the Lord by His other words—that whoever wishes to be greatest must be the least, and whoever would be the first must be the servant of all (Matthew 20:26–28; Mark 10:42–45; Luke

9042. *And with a blow shall have hurt a woman with child.* That this signifies the injuring of good which is from truth is evident from the meaning of "hurting with a blow" as injuring; and from the meaning of "a woman with child" as the formation of good from truth. For when a person is born anew, he is first conceived, afterward carried as if in the womb, and finally born. From this it is clear what is signified by a woman with child: namely, the state of the formation of good from truths. That the womb is where truth and good lie conceived may be seen above (n. 4918, 6433), and that to be in the womb and to come forth from the womb is to be regenerated (n. 4904, 8043); also that generations and births relate to faith and charity (n. 613, 1145, 1255, 2020, 2584, 6239). <margin>2</margin>The state of the formation of good from truths is also signified by a woman with child in Jeremiah: *Behold, I will bring them from the north country, and gather them from the sides of the earth, and among them the blind and the lame, the woman with child and her who is in labor together* (xxxi. 8). To be regenerated, the external or natural man must be in subservience and thus in agreement with his internal man. Specifically, going out from the womb is the movement from the internal man into the external or natural man, and birth is spiritual good—that is, the good of charity formed from the truths of faith—going out from the internal man into the external or natural. When good is in the natural man, then the person is a new person; his life is then from good, and his form is from truths derived from good; and he is like an angel, for the angels' life is from good and their form is from truths, which form is the human form; but this is a paradox to the natural man.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spiritual law "soul for soul" (lex talionis) from the previous page, explaining how this principle operates as a fundamental law of the spiritual world.</meta>

<page-num>280</page-num>
<header>EXODUS.</header>
<meta>[No. 9048. Continued]</meta>

The reason this law was given to the children of Israel is that such is the law in the spiritual world. Anyone there who does good to another from the heart receives a similar good. Therefore, anyone who does evil to another from the heart receives a similar evil. For good from the heart is connected with its reward, and evil from the heart is connected with its punishment; therefore, there is heaven for the good and hell for the evil. I have been allowed to know that this is so from much experience; for the situation is this: for someone who does good from the heart, good from heaven flows into their heart and soul from all sides and, by breathing into it, gives it life. At the same time, the affection of love for the neighbor to whom they do good is increased, and with that affection comes enjoyment—an enjoyment that is heavenly and inexpressible. The reason for this is that the good of love from the Lord reigns universally in heaven and flows in continually, according to the degree in which it is expressed to another. The case is similar in respect to evil. When someone does evil to another from the heart, evil from hell flows into their heart from all sides and, by provoking it, stirs it up. Then the affection of self-love is increased at the same time, and with it the enjoyment of hatred and revenge against those who do not submit themselves. The reason for this is that the evil of self-love reigns universally in hell and flows in continually, according to the degree in which it is directed toward another. When this occurs, punishing spirits are immediately present who mistreat the offender, and thereby the evil and its enjoyment are restrained.

<margin>2</margin> These things are so because the laws of order in the afterlife are not learned from books and stored in the memory, as they are with people in the world, but are inscribed on hearts—the laws of evil on the heart of the evil, and the laws of good on the heart of the good. For everyone carries into the afterlife that which they had ingrained in their heart during their life in the world—namely, evil with the evil, and good with the

<summary>This page explains that the law of "soul for soul" is a fundamental reality of the spiritual world, where good or evil actions from the heart naturally attract corresponding rewards or punishments. These spiritual laws are not external rules but are ingrained in the heart based on how a person lived their life in the world.</summary>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the law of retaliation (lex talionis) by discussing the law of order, which is the Golden Rule, and how Jesus' instructions on non-resistance are to be understood spiritually rather than literally.</meta>

<page-num>281</page-num>
# No. 9048.] CHAPTER XXI. VER. 22—27.

good. <margin>3</margin> The law of order from which these things flow is the law which the Lord taught in Matthew: *Whatever you would have men do to you, do also to them; for this is the law and the prophets* (vii. 12; Luke vi. 31). Order comes from the Divine truth that proceeds from the Lord. The laws of order in heaven are truths from good, and in hell they are truths separate from good. They are said to be separated, not on the part of the Lord, but on the part of man. Good is separated by its non-reception. <margin>4</margin> The law, which is called the law of retaliation, is described this way in Leviticus: *He who strikes a beast mortally shall make it good: life for life. If a man has caused a blemish in his neighbor; as he has done, so shall it be done to him; breach for breach, eye for eye, tooth for tooth: as he has caused a blemish in a man, so shall it be rendered to him. He who kills a beast shall make it good: and he who kills a man shall be put to death* (xxiv. 18-21). Because evil carries its punishment with it, the Lord said that evil must not be resisted. At the same time, it is explained how this law works in the spiritual world for those who are in good compared to those who are in evil, in these words in Matthew: *You have heard that it was said, "An eye for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth": but I say to you, do not resist evil: but whoever shall strike you on your right cheek, turn the other to him also. And if any man wants to go to law with you and take away your coat, let him have your cloak also. And whoever shall compel you to go one mile, go with him two. Give to him who asks you, and from him who would borrow from you, do not turn away* (v. 38-42). <margin>5</margin> Who cannot see that these words are not to be understood according to the literal sense? For who is bound to turn the left cheek to him who strikes the right cheek? And who is bound to give his cloak to him who would take away his coat? And who is bound to give his property to everyone who asks?

The subject there is spiritual life, or the life of faith, not natural life, which is the life of the world. The Lord here opens, in this chapter and the following, the interior things which are of heaven, but by means of such things as are in the world. The reason why He opened them by such things as are in the world was so that not worldly men, but only heavenly men, should understand. Worldly men were not meant to understand, lest they should profane the interior things of the Word; for by doing so, they would cast themselves into the most direful hell of all, which is the hell of the profaners of the Word. Therefore it is said by the Lord in Luke: *To you it is given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of God: but to the rest in parables; that seeing they may not see, and hearing they may not understand* (viii. 10). And in John: *Isaiah said, He has blinded their eyes, and He hardened their heart; lest they should see with their eyes, and understand with their heart, and should turn, and I should heal them* (xii. 39, 40). It is said, "lest I should heal them," because those who are healed and then return again to falsities and evils are guilty of profanation. These <margin>6</margin> are they who are meant in Matthew (xii. 43–45). But it will now be explained what is meant in the internal sense by the words of the Lord quoted above. In that sense, it deals with those who wish to use falsities to destroy the truths of faith—thus the spiritual life in a person when he is in temptations and under persecutions, and in good spirits when they are in infestations from evil spirits. By the cheek is signified affection for interior truth; by the right cheek, affection for truth from good; by dealing a blow is signified the act of hurting this affection; by a coat and cloak is signified truth in the external form (n. 4677, 4741, 4742); by going to law is signified an attempt to destroy; by a...

A mile signifies that which leads to truth, for the same thing is signified by a mile as by a way, and a way is that which leads to truth (n. 627, 2333, 3477); lending signifies instructing. From this it is clear what is signified by giving to all who ask—specifically, to confess everything regarding one's faith in the Lord. Therefore, the reason why evil should not be resisted is that evil does not harm those who are in truth and good, for they are protected by the Lord.

<margin>7</margin> These are the things that are hidden under the Lord's words mentioned above. This being the case, the Lord only says, *You have heard that it was said, An eye for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth*, and says no more; because an eye signifies the interior truth of faith, and a tooth signifies the exterior truth of faith, as will be seen in what follows. From these things it is clear how the Lord spoke when He was in the world; namely, that He spoke just as in the Word of the Old Testament throughout—simultaneously for the angels in heaven and for people in the world. His speech was in itself Divine and heavenly because it was from the Divine and through heaven. But the things He spoke were presented by means of things that corresponded in the world.

<margin>8</margin> What they correspond to is taught by the internal sense. That to inflict a blow or to strike the cheek means to destroy truths is clear from passages in the Word where mention is made of striking the cheek. And because in the genuine sense it signifies the destruction of truth, in the opposite sense it signifies the destruction of falsity; it is used in this sense by David: *You will strike all my enemies on the cheekbone; You will break the teeth of the wicked* (Psalm 3:7). In Micah: *They shall strike the judge of Israel with a rod upon the cheek* (5:1). And in Isaiah: *The bridle of him who causes others to go astray shall be on the jaws of the peoples* (30:28). 9050. That the soul signifies spiritual life is evident from the meaning of the soul as the life of man—specifically, the life of his faith, which is spiritual life. Throughout the Word, mention is made of the heart and the soul; the heart everywhere signifies the life of love, and the soul signifies the life of faith. Human beings have two faculties that receive life from the Lord: one called the will and the other called the understanding. Love belongs to the faculty called the will, for the goods of love constitute its life. But faith belongs to the faculty called the understanding, for the truths of faith constitute its life. However, these two lives in a person are still one; and when they are one, then the things belonging to faith also belong to love, because they are loved; and conversely, the things belonging to love also belong to faith, because they are believed.

<margin>2</margin> Such is the life of everyone in heaven. The reason why the life of love (or the will) is called the "heart" in the Word, and why the life of faith (or the understanding) is called the "soul," is because those who are in love to the Lord—and are called celestial—constitute the province of the heart in the Greatest Man (or heaven). Meanwhile, those who are in faith in the Lord, and thereby in charity toward their neighbor, constitute the province of the lungs (n. 3635, 3883–3896). This is why the "heart" in the Word signifies love, which is the life of the will, and the "soul" signifies faith, which is the life of the understanding (n. 2930, 7542, 8910); for "soul" [*anima*] in the original language is named after respiration,

9051. *Eye for eye.* That this signifies if anything in the interior understanding — they shall injure — is evident from the meaning of the eye, as the understanding, here the interior understanding, the life of which is the life of faith. Man has an exterior understanding and an interior understanding. The exterior understanding is where the thought is which comes to perception; but the interior understanding is where the thought is which does not come to perception with man, but yet does come to the perception of angels. This latter understanding is that which is enlightened by the Lord when man receives faith, for it is in the light of heaven, and in it is the spiritual life of man, which is not so manifest to him in the world, but is manifest in the other life, when man becomes an angel among the angels in heaven. Meanwhile that life lies concealed interiorly in the thought of the exterior understanding, and produces within it a holy and reverent feeling for the Lord, for love and faith in Him, for the Word, and for all other things of the church. The reason why the eye means the understanding is that the eye corresponds to the understanding, for the understanding sees by the light of heaven, but the eye by the light of the world. Those things which the former eye or the understanding sees are spiritual, and the field of its view is the knowledge acquired in man's

9052. *Tooth for tooth.* That this signifies if anything [is affected] in the exterior intellectual, is clear from the meaning of a tooth as the exterior intellectual, and therefore natural truth, for this constitutes the life of that intellect or understanding. Teeth have this meaning because, like a mill, they grind and thereby prepare the food which is to nourish the body, and here the food which is to nourish the soul. The food which nourishes the soul is intelligence and wisdom. This food is first received, ground, and prepared by the knowledge of truth and of good in the natural. That it is intelligence and wisdom which are called spiritual and celestial food may be seen above (n. 56-58, 680, 1480, 4792, 5147, 5293, 5340, 5342, 5576, 5579, 5915, 8562, 9003). From this it is plain on what basis the teeth signify the exterior understanding. What the exterior understanding is may be clear from what was shown just above (n. 9051) concerning the interior understanding. That it is interiorly in the voluntary is because a *wound*—of which we shall speak presently—signifies injury or extinction with reference to love exteriorly in the voluntary. For here, as in what precedes, the subject is the injuries which are done both in the internal man and in the external man, since the injury of the interior understanding is signified by the *eye*, and the injury of the exterior understanding by the *tooth*, and the injury of the power of truth of the internal man by the *hand*, and the injury of the power of the external man by the *foot*, as has been shown. Thus here the injuring of the voluntary of the internal man is signified by *burning*, and of the external man by *wound*. 9056. *Wound for wound.* That this signifies if anything of the affection of love which is in the exterior voluntary is injured, is evident from the meaning of "wound" as an injury of the affection which belongs to love; and because it belongs to love, it belongs to the voluntary, for love is of the will.
